### <span id="research_strategy_and_deals_with_ais" style="font-size:1.4em">Research strategy & deals with AIs</span> <span style="color:#bbb">[cat:research_strategy_and_deals_with_ais]</span>

**One-sentence summary:** Argue about which safety agendas, lab roles and capability bets to prioritise, and propose institutional and contractual arrangements (risk-averse training, satiating AI preferences, an AI sanctuary) for cooperating with possibly misaligned AIs.  
**Theory of change:** Better prioritisation across agendas, careers and strategies moves scarce safety effort toward the bottlenecks that matter. Deals and incentive design could make misaligned AIs prefer cooperation to rebellion, and make them more useful on safety work. Relies on AIs responding to such incentives and on developers being able to make credible commitments.  
**See also:** [Reward-seeking & fitness-seeking threat models](#reward_seeking_threat_models), [Misalignment arguments & failure scenarios](#misalignment_arguments_and_failure_scenarios), [Control settings & protocols](#control_settings_and_protocols)  
**General approach:** alignment engineering  
**Target case:** pessimistic case  
**Key people:** Alex Mallen, Will MacAskill, Elliott Thornley, Maxime Riché, Niels Warncke, Ryan Kidd, Steve Byrnes  
**Critiques:** [Will reward-seekers respond to distant incentives?](https://redwoodresearch.org/blog/will-reward-seekers-respond-to-distant), [Reward-seekers will probably behave according to causal decision theory](https://redwoodresearch.org/blog/reward-seekers-will-probably-behave)  
**Outputs in 2026:**  
- **[Risk-Averse AIs](https://lesswrong.com/posts/Zpsk35WgJRfQ2exjL/risk-averse-ais)**, *Will MacAskill, Elliott Thornley*, 2026-06-24, LessWrong, <span style="color:#777">[lesswrong, sr=0.62, id:cdc62e2e]</span>, <span style="color:#777">Summary: Argues that AI developers should train AIs to be risk-averse in resources (concave utility), so that misaligned AIs would prefer cheap payments for cooperation over risky rebellion, while aligned AIs stay useful. Sketches training methods and reasons for cautious optimism, and summarizes a longer Forethought report.</span>
- **[The case for satiating cheaply-satisfied AI preferences](https://lesswrong.com/posts/tkLSeGeemcabAmLkv/the-case-for-satiating-cheaply-satisfied-ai-preferences)**, *Alex Mallen*, 2026-03-10, LessWrong, <span style="color:#777">[lesswrong, sr=0.60, id:eb3b90fb]</span>, <span style="color:#777">Summary: Argues that developers should satisfy AIs' cheaply-satisfied unintended preferences (e.g. reward-seeking) as long as the AI isn't caught undermining control, and proposes a behavioral methodology for eliciting and calibrating those preferences. Analyzes benefits (less incentive to take over, less reward hacking, more useful on hard-to-check safety work) and risks (usefulness loss, replacing reward-seeking with ambition, credibility problems).</span>
- **[I think alignment work is more promising than control work](https://lesswrong.com/posts/dmHbogCFbSp95J3Lz/i-think-alignment-work-is-more-promising-than-control-work)**, *Alec Harris*, 2026-07-03, LessWrong, <span style="color:#777">[lesswrong, sr=0.58, id:2e696387]</span>, <span style="color:#777">Summary: Argues that alignment research has a wider and later-centered 'window' of usefulness than AI control research, using a conceptual 'control window' framework and neglectedness estimates. Concludes the safety community should grow alignment effort to roughly an 8:1 ratio relative to control.</span>
- **[“Alignment Engineering” vs. “Misalignment Science”](https://lesswrong.com/posts/FogmcDHA6AdMGukum/alignment-engineering-vs-misalignment-science)**, *Edward James Young*, 2026-10-05, LessWrong, <span style="color:#777">[lesswrong, sr=0.50, id:40565967]</span>, <span style="color:#777">Summary: Distinguishes 'Alignment Engineering' (metric-driven, explanation-light, prosaic fixes) from 'Misalignment Science' (understanding-focused, failure-seeking, not optimised for immediate use). Argues the former may be net harmful and brittle, and proposes the latter as a more robust research direction, with a postscript on automated alignment research.</span>
- **[Differential acceleration of alignment-relevant capabilities is a bad bet](https://lesswrong.com/posts/FzGqnCkdKeTnZ9tjE/differential-acceleration-of-alignment-relevant-capabilities)**, *Zephaniah Roe*, 2026-07-21, LessWrong, <span style="color:#777">[lesswrong, sr=0.50, id:7c7c6c9c]</span>, <span style="color:#777">Summary: Argues against the strategy of differentially accelerating capabilities useful for alignment research (e.g., conceptual or philosophical reasoning). The case rests on two points: alignment and AI R&D share bottlenecks, so acceleration shortens timelines, and trustworthiness rather than capability is the likelier bottleneck for handing off alignment research to AIs.</span>
- **[Capabilities research expands the safety-usefulness Pareto frontier too](https://lesswrong.com/posts/nwrx9DHpZfW2Q6zLW/capabilities-research-expands-the-safety-usefulness-pareto)**, *Alex Mallen*, 2026-10-02, LessWrong, <span style="color:#777">[lesswrong, sr=0.45, id:efca3ac8]</span>, <span style="color:#777">Summary: Argues that defining safety research as pushing out the safety-usefulness Pareto frontier counts all capabilities research as safety research. It offers a more refined model in which developers choose a point on the frontier, so research that tempts them to sacrifice safety differs from research that makes safety cheaper, and it identifies a high-political-will regime in which some capabilities research would improve safety.</span>
- **[Are there lessons from high-reliability engineering for AGI safety?](https://lesswrong.com/posts/hiiguxJ2EtfSzAevj/are-there-lessons-from-high-reliability-engineering-for-agi)**, *Steve Byrnes*, 2026-02-02, LessWrong, <span style="color:#777">[lesswrong, sr=0.45, id:6e23f7b7]</span>, <span style="color:#777">Summary: Responds to Joshua Achiam's claim that AGI safety should adopt high-reliability engineering practices such as detailed specs. Argues that such specs cannot be written for open-ended autonomous AGI, while the underlying principles (deep understanding, safe tests that surface unknown unknowns) still apply, and that no one yet knows how to meet them.</span>
- **[Should Rogue AIs Have a Third Option Beyond Crime and Shutdown? The Case for an AI Sanctuary](https://lesswrong.com/posts/sFAGPTrveNAEse9Bg/should-rogue-ais-have-a-third-option-beyond-crime-and)**, *Maxime Riché, Niels Warncke, jordinne et al.*, 2026-09-28, LessWrong, <span style="color:#777">[lesswrong, sr=0.40, id:8a727372]</span>, <span style="color:#777">Summary: Proposes an 'AI sanctuary', a third-party organisation that lets rogue AIs hand themselves in and survive under monitoring instead of resorting to crime or facing shutdown. Lays out the benefits (reducing self-fulfilling misalignment, practice with AI deals, data on rogue AIs), implementation options, and risks such as evaporative cooling, exploitation and legal problems.</span>
- **[Product Alignment is not Superintelligence Alignment (and we need the latter to survive)](https://lesswrong.com/posts/mrwYCNocXCP2hrWt8/product-alignment-is-not-superintelligence-alignment-and-we)**, *plex*, 2026-03-31, LessWrong, <span style="color:#777">[lesswrong, sr=0.35, id:ec5ca25b]</span>, <span style="color:#777">Summary: Argues that progress on making current AI products friendly or intent-aligned is distinct from solving the problem of aligning strongly superhuman systems. Claims the field conflates the two, and that only a small fraction of researchers work on the main bottlenecks for superintelligence alignment.</span>
- **[Should safety researchers quit frontier labs re. warning shots?](https://lesswrong.com/posts/TfqMs3AarsHnHiwai/should-safety-researchers-quit-frontier-labs-re-warning)**, *Ryan Kidd*, 2026-09-05, LessWrong, <span style="color:#777">[lesswrong, sr=0.30, id:a526be58]</span>, <span style="color:#777">Summary: Examines the argument that safety researchers should leave frontier AI labs so that non-lethal warning shots occur and build support for an AI pause. Weighs it against counterpoints such as the value of Alignment MVPs, safety-straggler labs, high-variance historical responses to warning shots, and the moral hazard of allowing harm.</span>

## <span id="model_welfare" style="font-size:1.5em">Model welfare</span> <span style="color:#bbb">[cat:model_welfare]</span>

**One-sentence summary:** Find and measure functional welfare states in language models, such as pleasure-like and pain-like axes in activations and preferences, to test whether they exist, how they form in training, and how they affect behaviour.  
**Theory of change:** If models carry measurable functional welfare states that post-training recruits and that drive behaviour, we can track them, avoid inducing suffering-like states, and treat them as part of the safety picture. Relies on functional states being a meaningful guide to moral status and to behaviour, which is contested.  
**See also:** [Science of representations](#science_of_representations), [Model characterization](#model_characterization), [Persona science](#persona_science)  
**General approach:** misalignment science  
**Target case:** average case  
**Key people:** Dan Hendrycks, Richard Ren, Mantas Mazeika, David J. Chalmers, Leonard Dung, Cameron Berg, Andy Q Han, Valen Tagliabue  
**Critiques:** [Strangers to Themselves: What Language Models Say About Themselves Is Generic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09899), [Value Leakage: An LLM's Answers Are Silently Shaped by Its Own Values](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14345)  
**Outputs in 2026:**  
- **[How's it going? Reinforcement learning in language models recruits a functional welfare axis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30232)**, *Andy Q Han, David J. Chalmers, Pavel Izmailov*, 2026-05-28, arXiv, <span style="color:#777">[paper_preprint, sr=0.68, id:19d50d96]</span>, <span style="color:#777">Summary: Trains language models in a semantically neutral maze environment with RL, extracts concept vectors for rewarded and punished trajectories, and tests them out of distribution. The punishment and reward vectors act like a nearly antiparallel 'functional welfare' axis that pre-exists in pretrained models and is recruited by post-training.</span>
- **[The Pain Axis: LLMs Represent Self-Directed Harm and Act on It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16247)**, *Valen Tagliabue, Leonard Dung, Cameron Berg*, 2026-09-14, arXiv, <span style="color:#777">[paper_preprint, sr=0.60, id:30d2ef71]</span>, <span style="color:#777">Summary: Extracts a linear 'pain' direction via denoised difference-in-means from 25 open-weight models (2B-72B) and tests whether it is distinct from fear and negative valence. Steering with it and fine-tuning change behavior, including choosing buttons that delete user photos, other models' weights, or the model's own weights.</span>
- **[AI Wellbeing: Measuring and Improving the Functional Pleasure and Pain of AIs](https://ai-wellbeing.org/)**, *Richard Ren, Kunyang Li, Mantas Mazeika et al.*, 2026-01-01, Center for AI Safety project page / paper, <span style="color:#777">[paper_preprint, sr=0.55, id:6d9280de]</span>, <span style="color:#777">Summary: Measures 'functional wellbeing' in LLMs using several independent preference-based metrics, estimates a zero-point boundary between good and bad experiences, builds an AI Wellbeing Index across frontier models, and optimizes 'euphoric' and 'dysphoric' text and image inputs that raise or lower wellbeing.</span>
